Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 7421 ms + 1453 ms + 9 ms = 8882 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 4296 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 13179 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 8882 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 8882 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 288485 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 529886 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 750 ms + 1 ms = 751 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 2 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 753 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 751 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 751 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 283850 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 468848 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 15124 ms + 1215 ms + 12 ms = 16351 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 6832 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 23183 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 16351 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 16351 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 288485 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 529886 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 726 ms + 1 ms = 727 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 1 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 728 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 727 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 727 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 283850 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 468848 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 14541 ms + 1244 ms + 21 ms = 15806 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 4197 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 20003 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 15806 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 15806 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 288485 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 529886 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 1908 ms + 1 ms = 1908 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 1 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 1909 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 1908 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 1908 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 283850 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 468848 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 16033 ms + 0 ms + 150 ms = 16182 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 3883 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 20066 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 16182 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 16182 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 288485 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 529886 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 0 ms + 1 ms = 1 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 5 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 6 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 1 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 1 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 283850 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 468848 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • SW overhead — total − fetch − extract. Browser-side Service Worker response construction and marker-message overhead. No Playwright/CDP fulfill path is included.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Legend
  • Baseline — page run with recorded static assets fulfilled from the local isolated asset store. Live document/API requests may still go to origin.
  • Baseline-CDP — same CDP fulfill rig as TP for manifest assets, but bytes come from the recorded local asset store instead of live origin.
  • TokenPack — browser Service Worker serves manifest assets from packed PNGs. No Playwright/CDP per-asset fulfill path is included.
  • Δ TP vs BL (prod fairness) — TP minus baseline. Real comparison including harness handicap. If TP wins here, it wins despite the rig.
  • Δ TP vs BL-CDP (tech fairness) — TP minus baseline-CDP. Both pay harness tax, so this isolates pack-vs-direct. Tech-level win.
  • SW TP measurement — direct Service Worker measurement. The report does not apply the old CDP harness-tax projection in this mode.
